MMWR:现场笔记 - 2026 年埃博拉病毒 暴发 的特征和监测。。。(刚果民主共和国) - 禽流感日记

MMWR: Notes From the Field - Characteristics and Monitoring of the 2026 Outbreak of Ebola Disease . . . (DRC) - Avian Flu Diary
新闻简报2026年5月,刚果民主共和国(DRC)发现了由本迪布焦病毒引起的埃博拉病毒暴发。截至2026年8月26日,暴发已导致5794例确诊病例和2786人死亡,成为历史上第二大埃博拉暴发。病死率为48.1%,暴发已蔓延至六个省,其中伊图里省占病例的84%。刚果民主共和国公共卫生部于2026年5月15日宣布了暴发,疾病预防控制中心于2026年5月20日启动了其紧急行动中心。尽管做出了努力,病例检测、接触者追踪和实验室检测等操作指标仍然低于目标,并且ZXQ​​TERMXQXZ继续快速扩大。CDC 的 MMWR 报告强调需要采取大规模公共卫生行动,以防止 暴发 成为历史上最大的疫情之一。暴发 仍在持续,并且仍然是国际关注的突发公共卫生事件。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发于2026年5月15日宣布,截至2026年8月26日,确诊病例5794例,死亡2786例。该报告于2026年9月3日发布。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在刚果民主共和国 (DRC),特别是在六个省:下韦莱省、上韦莱省、伊图里省、北基伍省、南基伍省和乔普省。它影响了这些省份 151 个卫生区中的 57 个。
事件
2026年5月,刚果民主共和国(DRC)发生了一起由邦迪布古病毒(Bundibugyo virus)引起的埃博拉病毒病暴发。此次暴发目前是历史上第二大疫情,截至2026年8月26日,累计确诊5794例,死亡2786例。美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)和世界卫生组织(WHO)正在监测并应对此次暴发。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年8月26日,累计确诊5794例,死亡2786例,病死率为48.1%。此次暴发已蔓延至六个省份,其中伊图里省(Ituri province)占病例总数的84%。CDC的MMWR报告警告称,需要大规模公共卫生行动,以防止此次暴发成为历史上最大的疫情之一。
应对、状态与不确定性
刚果民主共和国卫生部于2026年5月15日宣布此次暴发。美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)于2026年5月20日启动了紧急行动中心。公共卫生响应的运营指标仍低于目标,且此次暴发仍在迅速扩散。CDC和WHO正在监测此次暴发,并努力改进应对措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

MMWR: Notes From the Field - Characteristics and Monitoring of the 2026 Outbreak of Ebola Disease . . . (DRC) - Avian Flu Diary

News Brief
In May 2026, an Ebola disease outbreak caused by Bundibugyo virus was identified in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). As of August 26, 2026, the outbreak has resulted in 5794 confirmed cases and 2786 deaths, making it the second largest Ebola outbreak in history. The case fatality ratio is 48.1%, and the outbreak has spread to six provinces, with Ituri province accounting for 84% of cases. The DRC Ministry of Public Health declared the outbreak on May 15, 2026, and the CDC activated its Emergency Operations Center on May 20, 2026. Despite efforts, operational indicators such as case detection, contact tracing, and laboratory testing remain below targets, and the outbreak continues to expand rapidly. The CDC's MMWR report highlights the need for large-scale public health action to prevent the outbreak from becoming one of the largest in history. The outbreak is ongoing and remains a public health emergency of international concern.
Original source text (English)
Avian Flu Diary Covering Pandemic and Seasonal Flu, Emerging Infectious Diseases, public health, community & Individual preparedness. NOTE: All AFD blogs are written by a human - any mistakes are solely mine. Thursday, September 03, 2026 MMWR: Notes From the Field - Characteristics and Monitoring of the 2026 Outbreak of Ebola Disease . . . (DRC) Figure 1. Distribution of cumulative confirmed cases of Bundibugyo virus disease in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, as of 26 August #19,321 On May 15th Africa's CDC Convened an Emergency Meeting After Reports of a Large Outbreak of Non-Zaire Ebola In the DRC, which was later confirmed to be Ebola Bundibugyo. On May 20th the U.S. CDC issued a HAN (#00530) and 2 days later the ECDC released a Threat Assessment Brief. In June the CDC's MMWR released Modeled Scenario Projections for the Ebola Disease Outbreak Caused by Bundibugyo Virus, 2026 which warned that: `Large-scale, rapid public health action is needed to control the current outbreak, already the largest known BVD outbreak, from becoming one of the largest Ebola epidemics in history.' The most recent (Aug 28th) update from the WHO reports: As of 26 August 2026, a total of 5794 confirmed cases, including 2786 deaths, have been reported, corresponding to a crude case fatality ratio (CFR) of 48.1%. These figures demonstrate a substantial increase in the scale and geographic extent of the outbreak over the past three months. The crude case fatality ratio of 48% underscores the severity of the disease and ongoing challenges related to timely case detection, access to and quality of clinical care, and effective interruption of viral transmission. Delays in recognizing cases increase the likelihood of onward transmission within households, communities and healthcare facilities. The outbreak remains a public health emergency of international concern, following the advice of Emergency Committee meeting convened on 18 August. This week, the CDC's MMWR has published a new assessment which finds that this Ebola outbreak is now the second largest on record, and it continues to expand rapidly. Despite concerted efforts, too many cases are being identified belatedly - many being treated or dying at home or outside of treatment centers - which prevents timely contact tracing and helps to enable community spread. They report that `Nearly all operational indicators remain below identified targets (Table)'. While this MMWR report outlines areas where improvements are needed, efforts in that regard are complicated by an ongoing humanitarian emergency in the eastern part of the DRC which includes armed conflicts, limited healthcare infrastructure and availability, population displacement, and difficulty accessing affected communities. Notes from the Field: Characteristics and Monitoring of the 2026 Outbreak of Ebola Disease Caused by Bundibugyo Virus — Democratic Republic of the Congo, August 2026 Early Release / September 1, 2026 / 75 Please note: This report has been corrected. Dumazedier Kabasele1; Erika Meyer1; Issaka Kabore1; Amber Dismer1; Joelle K. Kabamba1; Anna Bratcher1; Constantin Kabwe Kola1; Carrie Eggers1; Delayo Zomahoun1; Noemi Hall1; Mutshiene Deogratias Ekwanzala1; Natalie Peters1; Amy Schuh1,2; Tara Sealy1; Philip Ricks1; Billy Mpianga Mutombo1; Michael Kinzer1; Benjamin A. Dahl1; Hyacinte Kabore1; Mary J. Choi1,2; John Rossow1,2; Sascha Ellington1; CDC 2026 Ebola Response International Epidemiology and Laboratory Task Force (VIEW AUTHOR AFFILIATIONS)View suggested citation Summary What is already known about this topic? In May 2026, an outbreak of Ebola disease caused by Bundibugyo virus was identified in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. What is added by this report? This ongoing outbreak is now the second largest Ebola outbreak in history. The targets for five critical public health response indicators (case detection alerts, contact tracing, laboratory testing, isolation of infected persons, and safe and dignified burials) have not yet been met, and the outbreak continues to expand rapidly. What are the implications for public health practice? Substantial improvements in established outbreak control measures are crucial to rapidly detect and diagnose cases and isolate and provide treatment for infected persons, prevent funeral-associated transmission to prevent additional spread, and control this rapidly expanding outbreak. The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) Ministry of Public Health declared an Ebola outbreak on May 15, 2026 (1). Two days later, CDC activated its Emergency Operations Center as part of the U.S. government response to this rapidly growing outbreak (2). This report describes the epidemiologic characteristics and monitoring of the ongoing outbreak in DRC. Investigation and Outcomes The 2026 Ebola DRC outbreak caused by Bundibugyo virus is now the second largest Ebola outbreak ever recorded. As of August 21, 2026, DRC reported 5,458 confirmed cases and 2,606 (48%) confirmed deaths. Compared with previous Ebola outbreaks, the increase in cases in DRC is unprecedented, with approximately 5,000 cases in 100 days (Ebola Outbreak: Current Situation | CDC). Cases have been reported from six of the 26 DRC provinces (Bas-Uélé, Haut-Uélé, Ituri, North Kivu, South Kivu, and Tshop), affecting 57 of 151 health zones in the affected provinces. Ituri province remains the outbreak epicenter, accounting for 84% of reported cases. Strategies known to control Ebola outbreaks include community-based surveillance, case detection alert notifications,* rapid and in-depth case investigations, identification and monitoring of contacts, infection control measures (e.g., prompt isolation of persons with suspected or confirmed Bundibugyo virus disease [BVD]), rapid diagnostic testing, mortality surveillance, and safe and dignified burials (SDBs).† Data Source Operational indicators for five domains have been generated based on experience with previous Ebola outbreaks, including DRC’s 2018 outbreak (3) (Table). Targets reflect the levels necessary to end the outbreak. The DRC Ministry of Public Health prepares publicly available daily situation reports, and CDC abstracts data from these reports to evaluate the established indicators each day. Indicator data are monitored over time to assess the outbreak trajectory. This activity was reviewed by CDC, deemed not research, and conducted consistent with applicable federal law and CDC policy.§ Operational Indicator Analysis Nearly all operational indicators remain below identified targets (Table). Operational indicator values were calculated for the 21-day period of July 31–August 21. The average percentage of alerts investigated within 24 hours (last reported August 5, 2026) was 83% (target = >90%). An average of 10.6 contacts were identified per confirmed case (target = ≥20), suggesting underreporting and underascertainment of case contacts. The percentage of confirmed new cases previously identified as known contacts (last reported July 12, 2026) was 15%–20% (target = >90%); this suggests that most cases are occurring outside known transmission chains. In addition, more than one half (59%) of confirmed Ebola deaths are occurring outside an Ebola treatment unit (ETU) (target = 0%), suggesting insufficient ETU capacity, fear of ETUs, and ongoing spread through unidentified transmission chains. Laboratory testing was performed for 72% of validated alerts (target = >90%), indicating that a substantial number of suspected cases remain untested. Test positivity was 24%, with a target of 0%. Although the national ETU bed occupancy was 64%, meeting the target of <80%, occupancy varied substantially by health zone, with some facilities unable to isolate all infected persons and reporting occupancies as high as 140%. Fewer than one half (49%) of affected health zones had at least one SDB team (target = 100%). Current data were not available for several response indicators, such as percentage of persons with confirmed BVD receiving prompt isolation (target = >90%) and percentage of deaths with SDBs (target = 100%), underscoring ongoing data gaps in this complex public health response. Preliminary Conclusions and Actions As of August 21, 2026, most operational indicator measures remained below established response targets, and data for others were unavailable, indicating gaps in surveillance, contact tracing, laboratory testing, health care–seeking, isolation, and SDB capacity that limit control of the ongoing outbreak. These missing data and operational gaps, together with continued geographic expansion of the outbreak, a high percentage of deaths occurring outside ETUs, and a low percentage of cases among persons previously identified as contacts, indicate uncontrolled expansion of the outbreak. Public health response activities are complicated by a protracted complex humanitarian emergency in the eastern part of DRC, including armed conflict, limited health infrastructure, population displacement and mobility, and constraints on access to affected communities. Containment and control of the 2026 Ebola disease outbreak requires integration and coordination of at least five response areas: 1) expansion of community-based surveillance systems ensuring rapid investigation of alerts; 2) improvements in contact tracing completeness and timeliness; 3) expansion of treatment and isolation capacity in affected health zones; 4) increased laboratory testing capacity, enabling prompt case identification; and 5) ensuring SDBs in affected health zones. In addition, collecting robust, high-quality data regarding these operational actions is essential at the health zone level; CDC’s continued support to the DRC Ministry of Public Health and partners with improving data collection is critical. Collecting data at the level of the health zone facilitates timely local outbreak response decisions. Rapidly enhancing international humanitarian coordination and mobilizing global technical, operational, and other needed support are critical for accelerating the response and controlling the outbreak. An outbreak of Ebola disease caused by Bundibugyo virus was identified in the DRC in May 2026. The outbreak is now the second largest in history, with 5794 confirmed cases and 2786 deaths as of August 26, 2026. The CDC and WHO have been monitoring and responding to the outbreak.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

刚果的埃博拉葬礼团队与社区愤怒、疲劳和感染恐惧作斗争

Congo's Ebola burial teams battle community anger, fatigue and fear of infection
新闻简报本文讨论了刚果东部Ituri省埃博拉葬礼团队在控制暴发疫情中面临的挑战。该暴发疫情已导致超过6,600例确诊病例中近3,200人死亡,并在包括叛乱暴力和人口流离失所等困难条件下迅速传播。由Yuma Adolphe领导的葬礼团队等正因传统葬礼习俗受到限制而与社区愤怒、疲劳和感染恐惧作斗争。这些团队处理仍含有活性病毒的埃博拉患者遗体,以防止进一步传播。然而,他们面临着因无法进行传统仪式而感到沮丧的逝者家属的攻击。卫生当局估计该暴发的真实规模是官方统计数据的三倍,而缺乏针对Bundibugyo病毒的批准疫苗或治疗药物使防控工作更加复杂。一线工作人员还因薪酬问题而要求更好的待遇,导致罢工,进一步阻碍了应对工作。世界卫生组织警告称,该暴发疫情可能超过历史上最致命的2014-2016年西非暴发疫情,后者导致超过11,000人死亡。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该暴发疫情自2026年5月中旬开始,Abineno Justine的葬礼于2026年9月3日星期五举行。
地点与对象
该暴发疫情集中在刚果东部的Ituri省,影响着当地社区和一线医护人员。
事件
埃博拉病毒病正在Ituri省迅速传播,葬礼团队因处理感染遗体的困难以及传统葬礼习俗的限制而面临社区敌意和疲劳。
规模、影响与风险
该暴发疫情已导致超过6,600例确诊病例中近3,200人死亡,卫生当局估计真实规模是官方统计数据的三倍。该疾病通过直接接触体液传播,而缺乏针对Bundibugyo病毒的批准疫苗或治疗药物进一步加剧了风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
葬礼团队在压力下工作,防控工作受到薄弱的医疗体系、不足的接触追踪和有限的实验室检测的阻碍。一线工作人员还因薪酬问题而面临罢工,局势仍处于调查中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Congo's Ebola burial teams battle community anger, fatigue and fear of infection

News Brief
The article discusses the challenges faced by Ebola burial teams in Ituri province, eastern Congo, as they work to contain the outbreak. The outbreak, which has killed nearly 3,200 people among over 6,600 confirmed cases, is spreading rapidly in difficult conditions, including rebel violence and population displacement. Burial teams, such as those led by Yuma Adolphe, are struggling with community anger, fatigue, and fear of infection due to the restrictions on traditional burial practices. These teams handle the bodies of Ebola victims, who still contain active virus, to prevent further spread. However, they face attacks from bereaved families who are frustrated by the inability to perform customary rituals. Health authorities estimate the true scale of the outbreak to be three times higher than official statistics, and the lack of an approved vaccine or treatment for the Bundibugyo virus complicates containment efforts. Front-line workers are also demanding better pay, leading to strikes that further hinder response efforts. The World Health Organization has warned that the outbreak could surpass the deadliest in history, the 2014-2016 West African outbreak, which killed over 11,000 people.
Original source text (English)
Skip to contentMenuToday's paper Long IslandSportsNewsInvestigationsRestaurantsOpinionBusinessThings to DoSchoolsObitsNewsday TVSearchTOGETHER LI Nation/World9/11 anniversary complete coverageLI high school football previewWallet Watch NewsNation/World Congo's Ebola burial teams battle community anger, fatigue and fear of infection Health workers who are members of the safe and dignified burial team disinfect a coffin to bury Abineno Justine, a 38 year old woman who died of the Ebola virus, in Bunia, Congo, Friday, Sept. 4, 2026. Credit: AP/Dieudonne Dirole By The Associated PressUpdated September 7, 2026 4:36 am Share BUNIA, Congo — Yuma Adolphe and his team are responsible for the safe and dignified burial of some of the people who die from Ebola in Congo's Ituri province, the epicenter of history's deadliest Ebola outbreak. Nearly every day, they dig the graves, prepare the bodies and handle the coffins. But as the authorities grapple with the outbreak, which has killed nearly 3,200 people among more than 6,600 confirmed cases, the burial team says they are at their limits, struggling with an increasing workload and hostile communities. “It is difficult, but we try to push ourselves beyond our limits,” Adolphe told The Associated Press. Since the outbreak began in mid-May, authorities have deployed small burial teams like Adolphe's across the hot spots to handle the burials. Paid $20 a day, they are a part of a group of front-line workers who are working to contain the rapidly moving disease. Despite the toll, the burial team members say the priority is to keep their community safe. Sign up for the Breaking News newsletter Get the latest breaking news as it happens. Sign up By clicking Sign up, you agree to our privacy policy. “We have to look at our own mental well-being, and physically the fatigue mounts, but we keep going to ensure the community is protected.” ‘In the past, we did not die like this’ But their work is in the crosshairs of the locals' frustration with the outbreak, which not only has taken their loved ones but prohibits them from traditional burial rites. Health workers who are members of the safe and dignified burial team prepare to bury Abineno Justine, a 38 year old woman who died of the Ebola virus, in Bunia, Congo, Friday, Sept. 4, 2026. Credit: AP/Dieudonne Dirole Health workers exclusively handle the bodies as Ebola spreads as a result of direct contact with the blood or bodily fluids of infected people. Experts say bodies of victims still contain considerable amounts of active virus and can spread infections further. Burial teams have faced attacks Since May, health workers and burial teams such as Adolphe's have been attacked by bereaved families across the front line of the outbreak. “Usually, the body is handed over to the family, who can handle it however they wish, in accordance with our African customs,” Adolphe said. “But in this situation, it’s difficult to let the family handle the body as they please. They are deprived of certain rituals, and that’s what leads to misunderstandings.” Health workers who are members of the safe and dignified burial team prepare to bury Abineno Justine, a 38 year old woman who died of the Ebola virus, in Bunia, Congo, Friday, Sept. 4, 2026. Credit: AP/Dieudonne Dirole Jeanne Alasha, adviser to the military governor of Ituri on health and humanitarian affairs, said that “as for those who continue to assault the teams, they are committing offenses punishable by law.” Ituri province, where the outbreak is concentrated, is also at the center of the rebel conflict in eastern Congo. The province is under a military government appointed by federal civilian authorities in Kinshasa. Shortly before noon last Friday, Justine Abineno, a chorister, was buried in Ituri. Abineno had died of Ebola. She had only complained of slight headaches before heading to a hospital and was diagnosed with Ebola, her mother said. A catechist presided over the ceremony attended by dozens of people, as a team of specialist burial team members in scrubs and boots stood by. Abineno's mother, Sofia Wanito, said that the doctors only allowed her to see the body through the window of the hospital ward, and she wasn't allowed to touch the coffin. “In the past, we didn’t die like this. I could not even cover her with the sheets myself and pay a final tribute,” Wanito said. Efforts to contain the virus are faltering The Ebola outbreak is spreading across six provinces in eastern Congo in some of the most difficult conditions imaginable, fueled by rebel violence, displacement and intense population movements. A weak healthcare and surveillance system also challenges responders, and insufficient contact tracing and laboratory testing. Efforts to contain the outbreak have also been complicated by the lack of an approved vaccine or treatment for the rare Bundibugyo virus causing it. Authorities have started vaccinating front-line health workers with the Evrebo vaccine, which the World Health Organization says could offer some protection against Ebola but there's no conclusive evidence that it would work against the current virus. Health authorities say the true scale of the crisis is likely three times more than official statistics as the disease continues to outpace efforts to contain it. WHO has said that it's projected to surpass the deadliest in history, West Africa’s 2014-2016 outbreak, which killed more than 11,000 people. Front-line health workers in eastern Congo have gone on multiple strikes to demand pay for their work, complicating efforts to stop the outbreak. ‘We are putting everything on the line’ Benjamin Muhindo, another burial team member, said that his family worries that he might bring the virus home. For him, though, a bigger priority is ensuring the proper burial of victims, he said. “Burials during this Ebola epidemic are very complicated, as we are working under pressure and constantly racing against the clock,” Muhindo said. Trauma experts say the high number of cases and the deaths are only one side of its impact at personal and communal levels. “The trauma of this outbreak doesn’t stop with the case count,” said Rose Tchwenko, Mercy Corps country director in Congo. The chain of transmission will only be broken by “an integrated holistic response,” which includes a social response to bereaved families and front-line workers, she added. After the burial on Friday, Adolphe said that he's very tired but more worried about the community than anything else. “We have no choice,” he said. “We are here to safeguard our community.” ___ Ope Adetayo reported from Lagos, Nigeria. ___ This story corrects the spelling of Abineno. The outbreak has been ongoing since mid-May 2026, with the burial of Abineno Justine occurring on Friday, Sept 3, 2026.
Location and population
The outbreak is concentrated in Ituri province, eastern Congo, affecting communities and front-line health workers.
Event
Ebola virus disease is spreading in Ituri province, with burial teams facing community hostility and fatigue due to the challenges of handling infected bodies and the restrictions on traditional burial practices.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak has resulted in nearly 3,200 deaths among over 6,600 confirmed cases, with health authorities estimating the true scale to be three times higher. The disease spreads through direct contact with bodily fluids, and the lack of an approved vaccine or treatment for the Bundibugyo virus exacerbates the risk.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

更快的诊断,更深入的洞察:研究人员加强应对2026年刚果民主共和国邦比布古约病毒(BDBV)暴发 - EurekAlert!

Faster diagnostics, deeper insights: Researchers strengthen response to Ebola outbreaks - EurekAlert!
新闻简报2026年9月,两项发表在《柳叶刀·传染病》上的研究强调了应对2026年刚果民主共和国邦比布古约病毒(BDBV)暴发的新方法。该暴发疫情始于2026年5月,暴露出由于缺乏BDBV验证检测方法而导致的快速诊断测试中的挑战。来自德国感染研究中心和德国灵长类动物研究中心的研究人员,联合由九个实验室组成的国际网络,开发了BDBV诊断的参考标准,并评估了四种PCR检测方法。他们发现没有交叉反应性,并证明了分散式网络可以加速诊断评估。此外,这些研究还探讨了已获批的重组VSV-ZEBOV埃博拉病毒疫苗是否能对BDBV提供交叉保护。来自十名志愿者的血清样本显示了针对BDBV的特异性中和作用,尽管其活性低于针对埃博拉病毒的活性。这些发现基于体外假病毒颗粒,需要通过真实BDBV进行确认,且不能证明疫苗对BDBV感染或疾病具有保护作用。这些研究强调了公共卫生机构与诊断公司之间合作的必要性,以提高对暴发的应对能力。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年9月报告了2026年邦比布古约病毒暴发疫情,该暴发疫情本身始于2026年5月。这些研究于2026年9月发表。
地点与对象
暴发 事件发生在刚果民主共和国,涉及一名在那里感染病毒的美国患者。这些研究涉及来自德国感染研究中心、德国灵长类动物中心和由九个实验室组成的国际网络的研究人员。
事件
研究人员开发了一种针对 2026 本迪布焦病毒 (BDBV) 暴发 的快速诊断测试,并研究了现有埃博拉病毒 疫苗 针对 BDBV 的潜在交叉保护作用。这些研究是在 暴发 期间进行的,并于 2026 年 9 月发表。
规模、影响与风险
2026 BDBV 暴发 引起严重出血性疾病,但没有获得许可的 疫苗 或治疗方法。研究表明,现有的埃博拉 疫苗 可能提供一些交叉保护,尽管中和活性低于 埃博拉病毒。研究结果表明需要进一步研究和合作。
应对、状态与不确定性
研究结果需要用真实的 BDBV 进行确认,并且不能证明 疫苗接种 可以保护人们免受 BDBV 感染或疾病。该研究强调了国际合作和监管途径对于 暴发 应对措施的重要性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Faster diagnostics, deeper insights: Researchers strengthen response to Ebola outbreaks - EurekAlert!

News Brief
In September 2026, two studies published in The Lancet Infectious Diseases highlighted new approaches to responding to the 2026 Bundibugyo virus (BDBV) outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. The outbreak, which began in May 2026, exposed challenges in rapid diagnostic testing due to the lack of a validated test for BDBV. Researchers from the German Center for Infection Research and the German Primate Center, along with an international network of nine laboratories, developed a reference standard for BDBV diagnostics and evaluated four PCR tests. They found no cross-reactivity and demonstrated that a decentralized network can accelerate diagnostic evaluation. Additionally, the studies investigated whether the licensed recombinant VSV-ZEBOV Ebola vaccine could offer cross-protection against BDBV. Serum samples from ten volunteers showed BDBV-specific neutralization, though activity was lower than against Ebola virus. The findings, based on pseudovirus particles in vitro, require confirmation with authentic BDBV and do not prove vaccine protection against BDBV infection or disease. These studies emphasize the need for collaboration between public institutions and diagnostic companies to improve outbreak preparedness.
Original source text (English)
Advanced Search Home News Releases Multimedia Meetings Login Register News Release 8-Sep-2026 Faster diagnostics, deeper insights: Researchers strengthen response to Ebola outbreaks Two studies on the 2026 Bundibugyo virus outbreak highlight new approaches to rapid diagnostics and vaccine preparedness Peer-Reviewed Publication German Center for Infection Research Facebook X LinkedIn WeChat Bluesky Message WhatsApp Email image: Colorized transmission electron micrograph of an Ebola virus virion. view more Credit: CDC/Frederick A. Murphy The current outbreak of Bundibugyo virus (BDBV) in the Democratic Republic of the Congo highlights the challenges of responding to rare but potentially devastating infectious diseases. Two studies led by researchers from the German Center for Infection Research (DZIF) at Charité – Universitätsmedizin Berlin and the German Primate Center – Leibniz Institute for Primate Research in Göttingen, together with other national and international collaborators, now provide important insights for outbreak response. One demonstrates how diagnostic tests can be evaluated rapidly during an outbreak; the other investigates the biological properties of the 2026 BDBV strain and whether an existing Ebola vaccine could offer some cross-protection. Both studies were published this month in The Lancet Infectious Diseases. Rapid evaluation of diagnostic tests During the COVID-19 pandemic, a research team at Charité – Universitätsmedizin Berlin led by Prof. Christian Drosten, a DZIF scientist, rapidly developed a PCR test for SARS-CoV-2 and made it publicly available within a short time. Because the assay’s performance had already been verified, manufacturers could replicate it and bring it to market quickly, enabling widespread testing from the first wave onwards. Since then, the EU has tightened regulations for new diagnostics, with each new test requiring its own validation study. This can slow the deployment of diagnostics during outbreaks. The 2026 BDBV outbreak exposed this challenge. BDBV can cause severe hemorrhagic disease, but unlike Ebola virus, there are currently no licensed vaccines or therapeutics specifically targeting it. The available certified Ebola test was not designed to detect BDBV, while no outbreak-strain virus isolate was initially available for test validation. On May 20, 2026, Charité admitted a US patient who had contracted BDBV in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. From a throat swab, researchers led by Drosten isolated the virus’s genetic material and developed a reference standard—a benchmark that diagnostic tests can be assessed against. The team then assembled an international network of nine laboratories, including members of DZIF, the German National University Medicine Network (NUM), and several European research consortia and encompassing major university hospitals and high-containment BSL-4 facilities. The network evaluated four candidate PCR tests from two manufacturers. Within two weeks, the tests underwent comprehensive analytical and clinical assessment, with no evidence of cross-reactivity. The study demonstrates that a decentralized network can provide robust performance data rapidly, potentially helping to accelerate regulatory approval and availability of diagnostic tests during outbreaks. The findings also highlight the need for close collaboration between public research institutions and diagnostic companies. Public funding could help accelerate the regulatory evaluation of diagnostic tests for rare, high-consequence pathogens, where commercial incentives are limited, says Drosten. Insights into the 2026 Bundibugyo virus and potential vaccine cross-protection The second study, led by DZIF scientist Prof. Stefan Pöhlmann at the German Primate Center (DPZ) in Göttingen, examined whether genetic differences in the glycoprotein of the 2026 BDBV strain might alter its ability to enter human cells. The glycoprotein is the virus’s most important surface protein and the key factor in its infectivity. Because work with authentic BDBV requires biosafety level-4 containment, the researchers used pseudovirus particles carrying the glycoproteins of BDBV strains from 2007–08, 2012 and 2026. They found no evidence that the 2026 virus enters human cells more efficiently than earlier variants. The researchers also investigated whether vaccination against Ebola virus could generate antibodies capable of neutralizing BDBV. Serum samples from ten healthy volunteers who had received the licensed recombinant VSV-ZEBOV Ebola vaccine were analyzed before vaccination and 28 and 180 days afterwards. Following vaccination, antibodies from the participants were able to neutralize pseudoviruses carrying the glycoproteins of all three BDBV strains, including the 2026 strain. BDBV-specific neutralization was detected in six of ten vaccine recipients at both time points, although neutralizing activity was around 3.5 to 3.6 times lower than against Ebola virus. The findings suggest that the licensed Ebola vaccine may provide some degree of cross-protection against BDBV and are consistent with previous studies in non-human primates. However, the current study used pseudovirus particles in vitro. The findings therefore require confirmation with authentic BDBV and do not demonstrate that vaccination protects people against BDBV infection or disease. Strengthening outbreak preparedness Together, the studies show how rapid diagnostic evaluation, virological research and immunological analyses can complement each other during an emerging outbreak. Establishing international laboratory networks, reference materials and regulatory pathways in advance could help shorten the time from outbreak detection to reliable diagnostics and potential preventive measures. Publications: 1. Best TD et al. Accelerated qualification of diagnostic tests during an ongoing outbreak of Bundibugyo virus disease: a collaborative analytical validation study. Lancet Infect Dis. 2026 Sep 2:S1473-3099(26)00465-2. doi: 10.1016/S1473-3099(26)00465-2. Epub ahead of print. PMID: 42685735. 2. Hoffmann M et al. Pseudovirus-based analysis of host cell entry and vaccine-induced neutralisation of Bundibugyo virus. Lancet Infect Dis. 2026 Sep;26(9):e330-e331. doi: 10.1016/S1473-3099(26)00407-X. Epub 2026 Jul 23. PMID: 42492560. 儿童必须以埃博拉 治疗 和预防研究为中心 - 医生无国界

Children must be centered in Ebola treatment and prevention research - Doctors Without Borders
新闻简报无国界医生组织 (MSF) 呼吁优先考虑儿童来研发与刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 当前由本迪布焦病毒引起的埃博拉 治疗 相关的医疗工具和 治疗。文章强调,与成人相比,儿童死于该疾病的风险要高得多。EBO-PEP 研究评估了口服 抗病毒药物 奥贝昔韦,由于缺乏儿科配方和片剂压碎或溶解的数据,因此排除了 44 磅以下(约 6 岁)的儿童。无国界医生敦促利益相关者支持儿科亚研究,并加速评估其他口服 抗病毒药物 和单克隆抗体作为暴露后预防的作用。吉利德科学公司已表示愿意提供儿科制剂,但具体时间仍不确定。文章强调,迫切需要确保儿童在 暴发 期间(而不是结束后)获得有效的预防性 治疗。
查看新闻五要素
时间
文章发表于2026-09-08。活动日期并未明确提及,但 暴发 自 2026 年 8 月 4 日起正在进行。
地点与对象
暴发 正在刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 发生。受影响的人群包括儿童,他们受到本迪布焦病毒的影响尤为严重。
事件
无国界医生组织 (MSF) 强调需要以埃博拉 治疗 儿童为中心进行预防研究,特别是针对刚果民主共和国当前的本迪布焦病毒 暴发。文章强调,儿童死于该疾病的风险较高,将他们纳入医学试验至关重要。
规模、影响与风险
与成人相比,5 岁以下儿童的 死亡率 率明显更高。EBO-PEP 研究评估了口服 抗病毒药物 奥贝昔韦,由于缺乏儿科配方和片剂压碎或溶解的数据,因此排除了 44 磅以下(约 6 岁)的儿童。文章强调了解决这一差距以确保公平获得医疗对策的重要性。
应对、状态与不确定性
无国界医生正在倡导将儿童纳入试验,并敦促利益相关者支持儿科子研究。吉利德科学公司已表示愿意提供儿科制剂,但具体时间表尚不清楚。文章还提到了针对被排除儿童的替代 治疗 方案的操作挑战。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Children must be centered in Ebola treatment and prevention research - Doctors Without Borders

News Brief
Doctors Without Borders (MSF) has called for children to be prioritized in research and development for medical tools and treatments related to the current Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), which is caused by the Bundibugyo virus. The article highlights that children are at a disproportionately higher risk of death from the disease compared to adults. The EBO-PEP study, which evaluates the oral antiviral obeldesivir, has excluded children under 44 pounds (approximately 6 years old) due to the lack of pediatric formulations and data on tablet crushing or dissolution. MSF is urging stakeholders to support pediatric sub-studies and accelerate the evaluation of other oral antivirals and monoclonal antibodies as post-exposure prophylaxis. Gilead Sciences has expressed willingness to provide pediatric formulations, but the timeline for this remains uncertain. The article emphasizes the urgent need to ensure children can access effective preventive treatments during the outbreak, not after it concludes.
Original source text (English)

See latest updates Children must be centered in Ebola treatment and prevention research In the ongoing Ebola outbreak in DR Congo, children with the disease are at a disproportionately higher risk of dying. September 1, 2026 Share Share in FacebookShare in BlueSkyShare in LinkedInShare in TwitterShare in Mail Hospital staff sing and applaud as 3-year-old Furaha is discharged from MSF's Ebola treatment center after recovering from the disease. | DR Congo 2026 © Alexis Huguet/MSF Related Democratic Republic of Congo Access to medicines Ebola Children’s health Outbreaks and epidemics View more Related Democratic Republic of Congo Access to medicines Ebola Children’s health Outbreaks and epidemics View more Children must be included in all research and development of medical tools and treatments for the current Ebola disease outbreak, said Doctors Without Borders/Médecins Sans Frontières (MSF) in a commentary published in The Lancet today. Specifically, children must have timely access to pediatric formulations for ongoing trials of oral post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP), including the oral antiviral pill obeldesivir that is manufactured by US-based Gilead Sciences. The current Ebola disease outbreak in Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), which is caused by the Bundibugyo virus, has disproportionately affected children. The proportion of children under 5 years old who were diagnosed and subsequently died from the virus is significantly higher than that of adults, as of August 4, among patients with confirmed cases whose age is recorded. Delayed inclusion in trials, lack of pediatric dosing guidance, and safety concerns often prevent children from accessing medical countermeasures like oral antivirals and monoclonal antibodies. We need to generate the evidence now so that children can access effective preventive treatments alongside everyone else, not after the outbreak is over. Dr. Neal Russell, MSF pediatric advisor Currently, the EBO-PEP study, which began on July 14, is evaluating the oral antiviral obeldesivir for preventing infection after high-risk exposure to Ebola disease caused by the Bundibugyo virus. If this medicine proves to be effective, it could offer a practical and scalable method to protect people who have had recent contact with someone with Ebola. While children weighing less than 66 pounds (approximately 12 years old or younger) have been excluded from the trial in its original design, we expect a planned protocol amendment to include children over 44 pounds will soon be accepted. However, due to the lack of an available pediatric formulation of obeldesivir and the absence of published data supporting tablet crushing or dissolution, children under 44 pounds who are age 6 or younger are still excluded from this study. While the trial is conducting a sub-protocol for those excluded — an alternative treatment regimen would be operationally very difficult in outbreak settings, especially for young children. Gilead has recently expressed willingness to make pediatric formulations of obeldesivir available soon, but it is unclear when a related clinical trial with oral formulations would start. Dr. Neal Russell, MSF pediatric advisor, said today: “Too often, the inclusion of children in the development of medical countermeasures to address public health emergencies is left to the ‘small print.’ But today — with children at a disproportionately high risk of dying if infected with Ebola in the current outbreak in DRC — their inclusion is paramount. "Researchers, donors, and other stakeholders must urgently support a pediatric obeldesivir sub-study, build on existing dosing guidance, and accelerate the evaluation of other oral antivirals and monoclonal antibodies as post-exposure prophylaxis. We need to generate the evidence now so that children can access effective preventive treatments alongside everyone else — not after the outbreak is over. "Gilead — the pharmaceutical corporation manufacturing obeldesivir — has committed to reproducing the antiviral in its pediatric formulation. We hope that Gilead will provide a clear and accelerated timeline for doing so, and that stakeholders can organize pediatric studies with this formulation — or with modified adult tablets as soon as possible — so that all children at risk can be included in studies evaluating which post-exposure prophylaxis options are most practical, timely, and effective.” An urgent opportunity to ensure children's access to oral post-exposure prophylaxis trials for Bundibugyo virus disease Read the commentary We speak out. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

埃博拉:为什么儿童在医疗危机中最后得到治疗 - dw.com

Ebola: Why children are last to be treated in medical crises - dw.com
新闻简报本文讨论了刚果民主共和国最近一次埃博拉 暴发 期间治疗儿童面临的挑战。报告强调,儿童比成人更容易死于这种疾病,五岁以下儿童的 死亡率 死亡率超过 60%。缺乏特定药物以及由于儿童症状类似于其他疾病而难以诊断儿童,导致了这种风险。一种实验性 抗病毒药物 药物奥贝昔韦 (obeldesivir) 正在针对 Bundibugyo 菌株进行研究,但 20 公斤以下的儿童并未包括在最初的试验中。德国的一个单独案例涉及一个家庭,在一名父亲感染病毒后接受了实验性抗体药物 MBP134 的治疗。虽然家人保持健康,但 治疗 在预防儿童感染方面的有效性尚不清楚。该文章强调了针对儿科的研究和配方的必要性,因为儿童的新陈代谢和对药物的反应与成人不同。对 暴发 的响应正在进行中,努力将儿童纳入 治疗 方案并开发靶向治疗。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发表于2026年9月6日,没有明确说明活动日期,但暴发于2026年5月中旬开始。
地点与对象
地点是刚果民主共和国(刚果民主共和国)。受影响人群包括儿童,特别是 5 岁以下儿童,以及受埃博拉 暴发 影响的普通人群。
事件
在刚果民主共和国最新的埃博拉病毒 暴发 中,儿童比成人更有可能死亡。本文讨论了治疗儿童所面临的挑战以及缺乏针对儿童的特定医疗 治疗。它还提到了正在为 Bundibugyo 埃博拉病毒 研究的一种实验性 抗病毒药物 药物奥贝昔韦,以及德国一个家庭接受实验性抗体药物 MBP134 治疗的病例。
规模、影响与风险
自5月中旬暴发开始以来,已有3000多人死亡。5岁以下儿童的死亡率率超过60%,体重20公斤以下的儿童不包括在治疗协议中。该文章强调了关于实验性 治疗 对儿童的影响以及管理中面临的挑战的有限数据。
应对、状态与不确定性
EBO-PEP 研究于 7 月中旬开始,目标包括体重超过 20 公斤的儿童。针对儿童的 治疗 选择仍然有限,MBP134 等实验药物在预防儿童感染方面的有效性尚不清楚。文章指出,有关这些药物对儿童影响的数据非常有限,其反应仍在调查中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola: Why children are last to be treated in medical crises - dw.com

News Brief
The article discusses the challenges of treating children during the latest Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo. It highlights that children are more likely to die from the disease than adults, with a mortality rate over 60% for those under five. The lack of specific medications and the difficulty in diagnosing children due to their symptoms resembling other illnesses contribute to this risk. An experimental antiviral drug, obeldesivir, is being studied for the Bundibugyo strain, but children under 20 kilograms were not included in the original trial. A separate case in Germany involved a family treated with an experimental antibody drug, MBP134, after a father contracted the virus. While the family remained healthy, the effectiveness of the treatment in preventing infection in children is unclear. The article emphasizes the need for pediatric-specific research and formulations, as children's metabolisms and reactions to medications differ from adults. The response to the outbreak is ongoing, with efforts to include children in treatment protocols and develop targeted therapies.
Original source text (English)
You need to enable JavaScript to run this app. Skip to content Skip to main menu Skip to more DW sites Latest videos Latest audio Regions Africa Asia Europe Latin America Middle East North America Germany Topics Climate Equality Health Human Rights Migration Technology Categories Business Science Environment Culture Sports In focus Saxony-AnhaltUkraineIran war Latest audioLatest videos Live TV Advertisement HealthDemocratic Republic of Congo Ebola: Why children are last to be treated in medical crises Hannah Fuchs 09/06/2026September 6, 2026 In the latest Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo, children are more likely to die than adults. But it's not always due to the disease. Specific medicines for younger people are often harder to come by. https://p.dw.com/p/5LgXj Copy link Children have been badly affected in the current Ebola epidemic, and treatment options for them remain limitedImage: Gradel Muyisa Mumbere/REUTERS Advertisement The current Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo has been devastating. Since the outbreak began in mid-May, more than 3,000 people have died. Almost half of people infected with Ebola are expected to die, and the mortality rate is particularly high for young children. According to a commentary by Doctors Without Borders recently published in medical journal The Lancet, the mortality rate for children under the age of 5 is over 60%. But often, it's because of a lack of medication and information on how to use it with young patients, rather than then disease itself. "Children must be centered in Ebola treatment and prevention research," the aid organization wrote in early September. At the heart of the debate is the EBO-PEP study, which began mid-July. The study looks at an experimental antiviral drug called obeldesivir, which is being studied for its ability to protect people exposed to the Bundibugyo Ebola virus, the strain responsible for the current deadly outbreak. But children weighing less than 30 kilograms (about 66 pounds) and around 12 years old or younger, were not included in the original study. But that could change soon with protocols to include children weighing over 20 kilograms. Children under the age of 5 are significantly more likely to die from Ebola fever than adultsImage: Benediction Murhabazi/AFP Children weighing less than 20 kilograms — that is, roughly 6 years old or younger — won't be included in the treatment. There is an alternative treatment for them: Another antiviral drug, remdesivir, given intravenously. But as Doctors Without Borders argued, a 10-day course of infusions is difficult to administer during an outbreak, especially for young children. According to Neal Russell, a pediatric doctor based in London who consults for Doctors Without Borders and who was also the lead author of the Lancet commentary, young children are at particularly high risk from Ebola. This could be due to their weakened immune systems, underlying health conditions, or the fact that treating them for Ebola is especially challenging in an outbreak. For example, children are often harder to diagnose than adults because early symptoms resemble other illnesses, and young children often find it difficult to describe their symptoms accurately. Other issues include the fact that children cannot always be accompanied into an Ebola treatment center by their parents or caregivers, something that is especially important in their recovery. Younger Ebola patients also require closer monitoring and specially trained staff. Ebola outbreak in DR Congo outpaces efforts to contain it To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video 02:14 Ebola in Berlin In May, an American doctor was treated at Berlin's Charite hospital after contracting the Bundibugyo Ebola virus while caring for patients. His wife and their four children were also considered at high risk. The medical team in Germany decided to treat the family prophylactically with an experimental antibody drug called MBP134. "Of course, a careful risk benefit assessment had to be carried out," said Han Ngoc Le, a doctor at the hospital who was involved in the treatments. The available data about the drug's impact on children was very limited. But at the same time, the children were at high risk of catching Ebola after close contact with their sick father. In the end, all the family remained healthy and no apparent side effects were seen. It's unclear whether the experimental treatment prevented infection in the children. Such a small sample can't be mapped onto the larger population either. But the test was valuable nonetheless. "The mere fact that the antibody was so well tolerated even in 1-year-olds and children under 12 is a valuable finding upon which future studies can build," Le said. Advertisement Why do such gaps in knowledge exist? Le explained that, from a medical perspective, children cannot simply be thought of as "small adults." Their metabolisms function differently, they react differently to medications, their pharmacokinetics — what a body does with a drug — are simply different. The same sort of cautions also apply to pregnant women. That's why dosages of medication can't simply be transferred from adults to children. It's also why any new medications require separate studies so that specific dosages can be determined and potentially special formulations can be developed. Children might require kid-friendly syrups instead of adult-size tablets, for example. DR Congo launches Ebola trial for high-risk contacts To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video 02:58 "All of this creates extra costs," said Marc Biot, access coordinator for Europe at Doctors Without Borders, who works to ensure people in need can get affordable, good-quality medical products. He said research institutions and pharmaceutical companies often see investing extra in pediatric studies as a lower priority. "It is faster and it can be cheaper if you only focus on adults," said Biot — which is why treating children and pregnant women usually only becomes part of the research at a later stage of drug development. "That is our plea — a plea not only to the scientists, to the pharmaceutical companies, but also to those who fund and allow trials to happen — to allow that to happen on the same speed for adults as for children and pregnant women." Biot said it's sad to see that pressure is needed to make it happen. "Whenever you start a clinical trial, you also should include pregnant woman or children," he argued. A difficult balance None of this is new. In 2020, the Global Accelerator for Pediatric Formulations network was founded — supported by the World Health Organization(WHO) — to accelerate the development and availability of child-friendly medicine worldwide. This isn't an issue limited to Ebola. Russell pointed to the 2024 mpox epidemic in the Democratic Republic of Congo, where children made up a large proportion of those infected or killed. Although a vaccine existed for mpox, it wasn't made for children. The WHO advised it be used "off label" for minors, but they tended to get it a lot later than adults anyway. Experts say they've observed similar patterns with other diseases including HIV, tuberculosis, and during the COVID-19 pandemic. When it comes to the development of medicines and vaccines, the first samples are often made for adult patientsImage: Thomas Lohnes/AFP/Getty Images "When you're doing a research study, which is not in an emergency, [it makes sense] to protect children and pregnant women from any potential harm of an investigational product," said Russell. "But when you're in an emergency, the balance between protecting those people from any potential harm from research, and protecting them from the very fatal disease, changes." Berlin-based doctor Le believes children and pregnant women should be considered right from the beginning of any new clinical trials. "They shouldn't be excluded from participation just like that," she said. "Instead, sub-studies or parallel studies should be set up from the outset." Russell calls for a similar approach. He wants to see all people benefit equally from scientific progress during a health crisis — especially in a health emergency, when the most vulnerable should not be the last to benefit from that progress. "No matter who you are, you should have the same opportunity to have access to these medical products," he said. This article was originally written in German. Together, these studies aim to prevent new infections, improve patient treatment, and deepen our understanding of the disease. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

非洲疾控中心警告刚果民主共和国埃博拉病毒暴发尚未得到控制 - bignewsnetwork.com

Africa CDC warns that the DRC's Ebola outbreak not yet under control - bignewsnetwork.com
新闻简报非洲疾病预防控制中心于2026年9月4日发出警告,称刚果民主共和国(DRC)的埃博拉病毒暴发尽管近期出现稳定迹象,但尚未得到控制。暴发至少自2026年7月13日起一直在演变,截至2026年9月1日已导致6,250例确诊病例和3,039例死亡,病死率为48.6%。共有1,439名患者康复。传播 在 51 个卫生区持续活跃,表明公共卫生风险持续存在。该报告基于刚果政府的最新情况更新,被认为是初步的,非洲疾病预防控制中心强调需要继续保持警惕和监测。
查看新闻五要素
时间
非洲疾病预防控制中心于 2026 年 9 月 4 日就刚果民主共和国埃博拉病毒 暴发 的持续状况发出警告。至少自 2026 年 7 月 13 日提到 Ruwanpala 埃博拉 治疗 中心以来,暴发 一直在不断发展。
地点与对象
刚果民主共和国 (DRC),特别是伊图里省布尼亚和 51 个卫生区。受影响人群包括参与 暴发 的患者和医护人员。
事件
非洲疾病预防控制中心警告称,尽管近期有稳定迹象,但刚果民主共和国的埃博拉病毒 暴发 尚未得到控制。截至2026年9月1日,暴发已导致确诊病例6,250例,死亡3,039例,病死率为48.6%。活跃的 传播 继续在 51 个卫生区开展。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年9月1日,刚果民主共和国已记录6,250例确诊病例,3,039例死亡,1,439例患者康复。病死率为48.6%。活跃的 传播 在 51 个卫生区持续存在,表明对公共健康存在持续风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
非洲疾病预防控制中心正在监测 暴发,但尚未宣布其已受到控制。目前情况仍在调查中,传播 活动仍在 51 个卫生区继续进行。该报告是根据政府最新情况报告编写的,目前的情况是初步的。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Africa CDC warns that the DRC's Ebola outbreak not yet under control - bignewsnetwork.com

News Brief
The Africa CDC issued a warning on September 4, 2026, stating that the Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) is not yet under control, despite recent signs of stabilization. The outbreak, which has been evolving since at least July 13, 2026, has resulted in 6,250 confirmed cases and 3,039 deaths as of September 1, 2026, with a case fatality rate of 48.6%. A total of 1,439 patients have recovered. Active transmission continues in 51 health zones, indicating ongoing public health risks. The report is based on the latest situation update from the DRC government and is considered preliminary, with the Africa CDC emphasizing the need for continued vigilance and monitoring.

combat EU announces 200-million-euro Greenland package Iran moves to restrict shipping in Strait of Hormuz Philippines VP Sara Duterte posts bail in threat case Africa CDC warns that the DRC's Ebola outbreak not yet under control A doctor cares for Ebola patients at the Ruwanpala Ebola Treatment Center, July 13, 2026, in Bunia, Ituri Province, Democratic Republic of Congo. /CFP The Africa Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (Africa CDC) said Thursday it remains too early to say that the Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) is under control, despite signs of stabilization in recent weeks. "We're still early to say that we're controlling the whole outbreak," Africa CDC official Wessam Mankoula told an online press briefing, noting that the outbreak "is still evolving." According to the latest situation report from the government, as of September 1, the DRC has recorded 6,250 confirmed cases and 3,039 deaths, resulting in a case fatality rate of 48.6%. A total of 1,439 patients have recovered. The outbreak has impacted 60 health zones across six provinces. Four health zones have successfully interrupted transmission by reporting no new cases for over 42 days, while another five zones are in a "control phase," having gone between 21 and 42 days without new cases. However, active transmission persists in 51 health zones, Mankoula reported. "For the last two weeks, we are seeing a decline in the number of cases; we are almost at the plateau phase," Mankoula said, cautioning that "it's very early to say we have sustained decline in this outbreak." Mankoula said the plateau could reflect stronger surveillance, earlier case detection, active case finding, infection prevention and control measures, and improved case management. "Hopefully, when we're following now the third week, we continue with this plateau, then we start seeing a decrease," he said. But he cautioned against drawing conclusions too soon. "We saw this plateau previously in June, then later on it's followed by an increase. This is why we are cautiously interpreting those data," Mankoula said. The current outbreak, which was declared in May, has become the largest and deadliest Ebola epidemic in the country's history and the second-largest globally, behind the 2014-2016 epidemic in West Africa. Mankoula also pointed out that, while Uganda recently declared its Ebola outbreak over, the ongoing active transmission in the DRC's eastern provinces bordering Uganda presents a persistent cross-border risk. He emphasized the need for robust surveillance systems at points of entry and in border communities to detect any potential spillover cases early and to prevent a new wave of cross-border transmission. "This outbreak is still evolving," Mankoula reiterated. "Incremental measures are no longer effective; it is time to scale up all different responses to control the situation on the ground." Africa CDC Director-General Jean Kaseya remarked on Wednesday that Ebola in the DRC "remains extremely serious and continues to spread." He added, "Despite considerable efforts by the DRC government and its partners, we are not yet where we need to be." Currently, there is no approved vaccine or treatment specifically targeting the Bundibugyo virus, which is the cause of the ongoing outbreak. Therefore, the international community is relying on experimental and repurposed medical tools. Regarding vaccines, the Ervebo vaccine, which is licensed for use against the Zaire ebolavirus, is being evaluated for cross-protection against the Bundibugyo strain through a field effectiveness study. Time
The Africa CDC issued a warning on September 4, 2026, about the ongoing status of the DRC's Ebola outbreak. The outbreak has been evolving since at least July 13, 2026, when the Ruwanpala Ebola Treatment Center was mentioned.
Location and population
The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), specifically in Bunia, Ituri Province, and across 51 health zones. The affected population includes patients and healthcare workers involved in the outbreak.
Event
The Africa CDC warned that the Ebola outbreak in the DRC is not yet under control, despite recent signs of stabilization. The outbreak has resulted in 6,250 confirmed cases and 3,039 deaths as of September 1, 2026, with a case fatality rate of 48.6%. Active transmission continues in 51 health zones.
Scale, impact and risk
As of September 1, 2026, the DRC has recorded 6,250 confirmed cases and 3,039 deaths, with 1,439 patients recovering. The case fatality rate is 48.6%. Active transmission persists in 51 health zones, indicating a continued risk to public health.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Africa CDC is monitoring the outbreak and has not declared it under control. The situation remains under investigation, with active transmission continuing in 51 health zones. The report is based on the latest situation report from the government, and the status is preliminary.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

"阻断埃博拉病毒入侵"... 加强并差异化应对刚果民主共和国及邻国的检疫措施 - 阿시아경제

News article (Korean original; verified English title unavailable)
新闻简报韩国疾病管理本部(KDCA)宣布对刚果民主共和国(DR Congo)及安哥拉、布隆迪、中非共和国、卢旺达、南苏丹、坦桑尼亚、乌干达、赞比亚、刚果共和国等9个邻国实施强化和差异化检疫措施,以防止埃博拉病毒病传入韩国。世界卫生组织于7月27日宣布乌干达暴发结束,但刚果民主共和国仍然是高风险地区,从7月中旬到上月底,确诊病例从1,926例激增至6,186例,死亡3,007例。暴发已扩展到6个省份,超过了2018-2020年的疫情。由于正在进行的 暴发,KDCA 将刚果民主共和国评估为“非常高”风险,并将其邻国评估为“高风险”。接受接触者管理的确诊病例比例仍低于25%,引发人们对进一步传播的担忧。作为回应,KDCA 将通过 DUR-ITS 系统为来自这些国家的旅客实施健康状况报告、短信通知和 监测。对于直接从刚果民主共和国入境的人员,将进行登机检疫筛查和主动监测。肯尼亚已从名单中删除,因为它现在被视为“低风险”,并且不再与 暴发 国家接壤。
查看新闻五要素
时间
容器 亚洲商报 开放导航 股票经济 房地产业·IT政治学会 世界文化·生活观点·社论视觉新闻学会“阻止埃博拉入境”。
地点与对象
刚果民主共和国(DR Congo)和九个邻国(安哥拉、布隆迪、中非共和国、卢旺达、南苏丹、坦桑尼亚、乌干达、赞比亚、刚果共和国)。
事件
韩国疾病管理和预防机构(KDCA)宣布对刚果民主共和国及其邻国采取加强和差异化的检疫措施,以防止埃博拉病毒病传入韩国。世界卫生组织于7月27日宣布乌干达暴发结束,但由于刚果民主共和国正在进行暴发,KDCA将刚果民主共和国及其邻国评估为高风险。
规模、影响与风险
从7月中旬到上月底,刚果民主共和国确诊病例从1926例激增至6186例,死亡3007人。暴发已扩展到6个省份,超过了2018-2020年的疫情。世界卫生组织将刚果民主共和国评估为“非常高”风险,将其邻国评估为“高风险”。接受接触者管理的确诊病例比例仍低于25%,引发人们对进一步传播到邻国的担忧。
应对、状态与不确定性
KDCA将把刚果民主共和国及其周边9个国家作为重点检疫管理区进行管理,通过DUR-ITS系统实施健康状况报告、短信通知和监测。对于来自刚果民主共和国的旅客,将进行登机口检疫筛查和主动监测。肯尼亚已从名单中删除,因为它现在被评估为“低风险”,并且不再与 暴发 国家接壤。

News article (Korean original; verified English title unavailable)

Original title (Korean)
"Blocking Ebola Entry"... Strengthened and Differentiated Quarantine for DR Congo and Neighboring Countries - 아시아경제
News Brief
The Korea Disease Control and Prevention Agency (KDCA) has announced strengthened and differentiated quarantine measures for the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DR Congo) and nine neighboring countries, including Angola, Burundi, the Central African Republic, Rwanda, South Sudan, Tanzania, Uganda, Zambia, and the Republic of the Congo, to prevent the introduction of Ebola virus disease into Korea. The WHO declared the end of the outbreak in Uganda on July 27, but DR Congo remains a high-risk area with confirmed cases surging from 1,926 to 6,186 and 3,007 deaths from mid-July to the end of last month. The outbreak has expanded to six provinces, surpassing the 2018-2020 epidemic. The KDCA has assessed DR Congo as "very high" risk and its neighbors as "high risk" due to the ongoing outbreak. The proportion of confirmed cases under contact management remains below 25%, raising concerns about further spread. In response, the KDCA will implement health status reporting, SMS notifications, and surveillance via the DUR-ITS system for travelers from these countries. For those entering directly from DR Congo, gate quarantine screening and active monitoring will be conducted. Kenya has been removed from the list as it is now considered "low risk" and no longer shares a border with an outbreak country.
Original source text (Korean)

Strengthened and Differentiated Quarantine for DR Congo and Neighboring Countries Language Language Close KR CN Open Search bar Search field News Search Remove icon Search icon Dim Society Social General "Blocking Ebola Entry"... Strengthened and Differentiated Quarantine for DR Congo and Neighboring Countries by Lee Sungmin Published 07 Sep.2026 09:32(KST) Share Language Text Size Print Confirmed Cases in DR Congo Triple in Two Months All Travelers from Neighboring Countries Required to Report Health Status Gate Quarantine Screening and Active Monitoring for Entrants from DR Congo The Korea Disease Control and Prevention Agency (KDCA) announced on September 7 that it has designated the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DR Congo) and nine neighboring countries as key quarantine management areas for Ebola virus disease, adjusting quarantine measures by country according to each nation's risk level to proactively respond to the possible introduction of Ebola virus disease into Korea. From mid-July to the end of last month, the number of confirmed cases in DR Congo surged from 1,926 to 6,186, with 3,007 deaths. The outbreak has expanded to six provinces, surpassing the Ebola virus disease epidemic that occurred between 2018 and 2020. However, the proportion of confirmed cases under contact management remains below 25%, fueling concerns about further spread to neighboring countries. View original image The World Health Organization (WHO) officially declared the end of the outbreak in Uganda on July 27, 42 days after the last patient was discharged and no additional cases were reported. However, since Uganda shares a border with DR Congo, there is a possibility of reintroduction. In this context, the WHO assessed the risk level for DR Congo as "very high" and, via an interim recommendation issued on July 24, assessed Angola, Burundi, the Central African Republic, Rwanda, South Sudan, Tanzania, Uganda, Zambia, and the Republic of the Congo—all countries sharing land borders with DR Congo—as "high risk." The KDCA will manage DR Congo and the nine neighboring countries, all assessed as high risk, as key quarantine management areas. However, in consideration of the varying risks of introduction and transmission between neighboring countries and the main outbreak country, it plans to establish a dual quarantine response system tailored to those differences. Entry procedures will require arrivals who have visited or stayed in any of the nine outbreak-risk countries, including Uganda and South Sudan, to report their health status in full using the Q-Code system (or a health status questionnaire). For travelers entering directly from DR Congo, where a large-scale outbreak is ongoing, pre-arrival passenger lists will be obtained, and targeted screening will be conducted at arrival gates. These individuals will also be subject to active monitoring after entry, in line with the virus's maximum incubation period (21 days). Kenya will be removed from the list of quarantine management areas, as it is now evaluated as "low risk" and no longer shares a border with an outbreak country following Uganda's declaration of Ebola outbreak termination. Meanwhile, Ethiopia will continue to be managed as a key quarantine management area because it is the largest air transit hub on the African continent and the only country with a direct flight to Korea; therefore, most travelers from high-risk areas enter Korea via Ethiopia, making it a “direct flight high-risk area.” Hot Picks Today Surging 73% in a Month, But "Still More Upside"... Im Seungkwan, Commissioner of the Korea Disease Control and Prevention Agency, stated, "Although the current domestic risk of Ebola introduction is not high, these measures represent a proactive response to focus disease control resources according to changing risks while closely monitoring the spread of the Ebola outbreak in Africa." He added, "We will implement health status reporting, distribute SMS notifications to all travelers from the ten countries, and activate surveillance via the overseas travel history information system for medical institutions (DUR-ITS). For entrants from DR Congo, we will add gate quarantine screening and active monitoring to ensure thorough management." 한글 기사 보기 This content was produced with the assistance of AI translation services. © The Asia Business Daily. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

世卫组织谭德塞表示,必须扩大埃博拉应对措施以应对刚果 暴发 -sightmagazine.com.au

WHO’s Tedros says Ebola response must be scaled up to tackle Congo outbreak - sightmagazine.com.au
新闻简报世界卫生组织 (WHO) 总干事谭德塞于 2026 年 9 月 3 日呼吁加大刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 埃博拉疫情应对力度。由本迪布焦病毒株引起的 暴发 疫情已导致超过 3,000 人死亡,目前是刚果民主共和国历史上第二大疫情,仅次于 2018-2020 年的疫情。这也是该国历史上最致命的 暴发。世界卫生组织已将检测能力提高到每天 3,000 次检测,并运送了 320 吨物资,但需要进一步的资金来扩大 治疗 中心的床位容量。疫苗和治疗的临床试验正在进行中,目标是在10月或11月开始疗效试验。然而,许多死亡者不在已知的联系人名单上,这表明存在身份不明的 传播 链。谭德塞强调,由于人道主义援助资金已经收缩,因此需要增加国际资金来支持应对工作。由于不安全的埋葬方式以及缺乏已证实对 Bundibugyo 菌株有效的 治疗 或 疫苗,暴发 仍然是重大的公共卫生威胁。
查看新闻五要素
时间
世界卫生组织 (WHO) 总干事谭德塞于 2026 年 9 月 3 日发表了上述声明。刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 的 暴发 至少自 2026 年 5 月 31 日以来一直在进行。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在刚果民主共和国,特别是东北地区,包括伊图里省。受影响的人群包括该地区的人和曾接触过本迪布焦病毒株的人。
事件
世卫组织总干事谭德塞强调,需要加大埃博拉应对力度,以解决刚果民主共和国的 暴发 问题。本迪布焦病毒株是 暴发 病毒的罪魁祸首,该病毒已成为刚果历史上规模最大、最致命的病毒。世界卫生组织正在对潜在的 疫苗 和 治疗 进行临床疗效试验,并与合作伙伴合作,从 10 月或 11 月开始在刚果民主共和国开展所有三种 疫苗 的疗效试验。
规模、影响与风险
刚果民主共和国埃博拉暴发死亡人数已超过3000人。暴发 现在仅次于 2014-2016 西非 暴发。世界卫生组织已运送了 320 吨物资,并将检测能力提高到每天 3000 次检测。然而,许多死亡者不在已知接触者名单上,这表明 传播 链尚未确定。由于不安全的埋葬方式以及需要进一步的资金来增加 治疗 中心的床位容量,风险仍然很高。
应对、状态与不确定性
世界卫生组织正在加紧努力遏制 暴发,包括 疫苗 和 治疗 的临床试验。由于目前的应对措施还不够,迫切需要为刚果政府主导的应对计划提供资金。暴发 仍在调查中,追踪 传播 链面临挑战。世界卫生组织尚未证实任何 疫苗 或 治疗 对本迪布焦病毒株的有效性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

WHO’s Tedros says Ebola response must be scaled up to tackle Congo outbreak - sightmagazine.com.au

News Brief
The World Health Organization (WHO) director-general, Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus, called for scaling up the Ebola response in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) on 3 September 2026. The outbreak, caused by the Bundibugyo virus strain, has surpassed 3,000 deaths and is now the second-largest in DRC’s history, following the 2018-2020 epidemic. It is also the deadliest outbreak in the country’s history. The WHO has increased testing capacity to 3,000 tests per day and shipped 320 tons of supplies, but further funding is needed to expand bed capacity at treatment centers. Clinical trials for vaccines and treatments are underway, with the aim of starting efficacy trials in October or November. However, many deaths are among people not on known contact lists, indicating unidentified transmission chains. Tedros highlighted the need for increased international funding to support the response, as financing for humanitarian assistance has contracted. The outbreak remains a significant public health threat due to unsafe burial practices and the lack of confirmed effective treatments or vaccines for the Bundibugyo strain.
Original source text (English)

Reviews On The Screen Music Books Casting the net App reviews Podcasts Lifestyle Origins Know it all Helpdesk Trendspotter Significant Sights GreenSight Health Insight A Sustainable Life Great Works Table Talk 20/20 Snapshot The Big Picture Our Team David Adams Lloyd Harkness Jenny Baxter Marcus Cheong Eduardo Campos Lima Paul Clark Samantha Elley Mal Fletcher David Goodwin Jo Kadlecek Nils von Kalm Tonny Onyulo Angela Youngman WHO’s Tedros says Ebola response must be scaled up to tackle Congo outbreak OLIVIA LE POIVEDIN David Adams September 3, 2026 No Comments Geneva, Switzerland Reuters The head of the World Health Organization on Wednesday said the Ebola response needs to be scaled up to tackle the outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo, with tracking the chains of transmission still posing a challenge. “Until every chain is found and broken, the epidemic will continue and will continue to pose a threat to DRC, its neighbours and the region as a whole,” the WHO’s director-general, Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus, told reporters in Geneva. Director-General of the World Health Organisation (WHO) Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus tours the Evangelical Medical Center (CEM), one of the facilities at the forefront of the response to the Ebola outbreak during his visit to coordinate the response to the Ebola outbreak, as agencies intensify efforts to contain a new Ebola outbreak caused by the Bundibugyo virus strain, in Bunia, Ituri province, Democratic Republic of Congo, on 31st May, 2026. PICTURE: Reuters/Gradel Muyisa Mumbere/File photo The number of deaths in Congo’s Ebola outbreak has surpassed 3,000, government data showed on Wednesday, as the country’s worst epidemic continues to spread across its northeast. The outbreak, caused by the Bundibugyo species of Ebola, became the largest and deadliest in Congo’s history after surpassing the 2018-2020 epidemic. It is now second only to the 2014-2016 West African outbreak, which infected over 28,600 people and killed more than 11,000 across Guinea, Liberia and Sierra Leone. But there is now hope of possible progress in treatments and vaccinations. The WHO aims to start clinical efficacy trials of potential vaccines next month or in November, and trials on treatments are under way. We rely on our readers to fund Sight's work - become a financial supporter today! For more information, head to our Subscriber's page. Funding needed for more beds However, Tedros said many deaths were among people who were not on a list of known contacts. “That means there are chains of transmission we don’t know about,” he said, adding that many of those who died were still being buried unsafely, which is fuelling transmission. He urged the international community to increase and accelerate funding for the Congolese government-led Ebola response plan, which is seeking $US1.3 billion. While the WHO has managed to ship 320 tons of supplies, and has increased testing capacity to 3,000 tests per day, it needs further funding to help support its response – including to increase bed capacity at Ebola treatment centres. Keep up-to-date with the latest news - subscribe to the free Sight Daily newsletter Luca Fontana, technical officer for health and technical logistics with the WHO, said it costs $US15 million per month for a 1,500 bed treatment centre. “We know that we are making this request at a time when financing for humanitarian assistance has contracted,” Tedros said. “But as we know from previous Ebola outbreaks, we can control this epidemic if we choose to.” Clinical trials More than 300 Ebola patients have been enrolled for a trial of two treatments, Tedros said. Trials are also under way of the antiviral drug obeldesivir among people considered high-risk contacts of Ebola cases, to assess if it is effective in preventing disease after exposure to the virus. Two new vaccines against Bundibugyo virus are in safety trials in Britain and Canada. It is not known whether an approved vaccine against the Zaire Ebola virus would be effective against Bundibugyo virus, so it requires clinical efficacy trials, the WHO said. “WHO is working with partners to progress all three vaccines to efficacy trials in DRC, starting in October or November,” Tedros said. View five news elements
Time
The World Health Organization (WHO) director-general, Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus, made the statement on 3 September 2026. The outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) has been ongoing since at least 31 May 2026.
Location and population
The outbreak is occurring in the Democratic Republic of Congo, specifically in the northeast region, including Ituri province. The affected population includes people in the region and those who have been exposed to the Bundibugyo virus strain.
Event
WHO’s director-general, Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus, emphasized the need to scale up the Ebola response to address the outbreak in the DRC. The Bundibugyo virus strain is responsible for the outbreak, which has become the largest and deadliest in Congo’s history. The WHO is conducting clinical efficacy trials of potential vaccines and treatments, and is working with partners to progress all three vaccines to efficacy trials in the DRC starting in October or November.
Scale, impact and risk
The number of deaths in the DRC’s Ebola outbreak has surpassed 3,000. The outbreak is now second only to the 2014-2016 West African outbreak. The WHO has shipped 320 tons of supplies and increased testing capacity to 3,000 tests per day. However, many deaths are among people not on a list of known contacts, indicating chains of transmission that are not yet identified. The risk remains high due to unsafe burial practices and the need for further funding to increase bed capacity at treatment centers.
Response, status and uncertainty
The WHO is intensifying efforts to contain the outbreak, including clinical trials of vaccines and treatments. Funding for the Congolese government-led response plan is urgently needed, as the current response is insufficient. The outbreak is still under investigation, with challenges in tracking transmission chains. The WHO has not yet confirmed the effectiveness of any vaccine or treatment against the Bundibugyo virus strain.
原文 / Source
Published: 2026-09-06  |  Publisher: ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

American doctor tests positive for Ebola in Democratic Republic of Congo outbreak - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

News Brief
An American doctor working in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) has tested positive for Ebola, according to an international Christian missions organization. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) confirmed the case but did not identify the individual. The patient, Peter Stafford, was exposed while treating patients at Nyankunde Hospital and developed symptoms over the weekend, testing positive late Sunday. The CDC has activated its Emergency Operations Center and is deploying technical experts to manage the situation. The agency is also coordinating with airlines and international partners to identify and manage travelers with possible Ebola exposure. The U.S. State Department is working with the CDC and military on potential repatriation of affected Americans. The CDC has prepared to restrict entry for travelers from the DRC, Uganda, and South Sudan who have been in those areas in the previous 21 days. As of Sunday, the DRC reported 10 confirmed cases and 336 suspected cases of Ebola, with 88 suspected deaths. The CDC emphasized that the risk to the U.S. general public remains low. The World Health Organization (WHO) classified the outbreak in the DRC and Uganda as a 'public health emergency of international concern.'.

OffOn Stream on American doctor tests positive for Ebola in Democratic Republic of Congo outbreak The patient developed symptoms over the weekend and tested positive late Sunday. ByYouri Benadjaoud, Jack Moore, Eric M. Strauss, David Brennan, and Mary Kekatos May 18, 2026, 10:40 PM An American doctor working in the Democratic Republic of the Congo has tested positive for Ebola amid the outbreak, according to an international Christian missions organization. Dr. Peter Stafford, a medical missionary with the missions organization Serge, was exposed while treating patients at Nyankunde Hospital, the group said Monday. He sought testing "after presenting symptoms consistent with the virus," Serge said in a statement. Serge, an international Christian missions organization, confirmed today that medical missionary Dr. Peter Stafford, pictured with his wife Rebeka, has tested positive for the Bundibugyo ebolavirus variant in Bunia, Democratic Republic of Congo. Serge Stafford has served at Nyankunde Hospital with Serge since 2023, according to the organization. Two other Serge medical missionaries, including Stafford's wife, were treating patients in the region when the outbreak began and remain asymptomatic, Serge said Monday. "Our medical teams labor in some of the most demanding settings in the world, serving vulnerable communities who have limited access to healthcare," Joel Hylton, Serge's senior director of mission, said in a statement. "We are profoundly grateful for their dedication to the people of the DRC, and we deeply lament the hardship they are enduring under this current threat. Our concern extends equally to our Congolese colleagues and friends in the region who face these same risks." The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) confirmed Monday that at least one American working in the Democratic Republic of the Congo has tested positive for Ebola, but did not identify that person. Dr. Satish K. Pillai, incident manager for the CDC's Ebola response, told reporters that the individual developed symptoms over the weekend and tested positive late Sunday. Pillai added that the patient and six other high-risk contacts are being moved to Germany for care and stressed that the risk to the U.S. general public remains low. Related Ebola outbreak in Africa is 'public health emergency of international concern,' WHO says "Given the previous experience for caring for Ebola patients, coupled with the flight times being significantly shorter, this allows us to get these persons to points of care quickly," Pillai said. Pillai said the CDC has activated its Emergency Operations Center through its country offices in the DRC and in Uganda, and is deploying technical experts that have been requested from Atlanta headquarters. The CDC said earlier Monday that it is preparing to restrict entry for travelers arriving from parts of central Africa where an Ebola outbreak has been declared, in coordination with the Department of Homeland Security. Non-U.S. passport holders will face entry restrictions if they have been in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Uganda or South Sudan in the previous 21 days. The move is being carried out under Title 42 of the Public Health Services Act, which allows the CDC director to suspend entry of individuals into the U.S. to protect public health. A staff member of the CBCA Virunga Hospital checks a visitor's temperature using a a contactless infrared thermometer, in Goma, Democratic Republic of Congo, on May 17, 2026. Jospin Mwisha/AFP via Getty Images The order will be in effect for 30 days and does not apply to U.S. citizens or lawful permanent residents. On Sunday, the CDC said in a statement that a "small number of Americans" are directly affected by an Ebola outbreak occurring in the Democratic Republic of Congo. Related Everything you need to know about Ebola On Saturday, the World Health Organization said in a statement that the Ebola outbreak in the DRC and Uganda constituted a "public health emergency of international concern." As of Sunday, there were 10 confirmed Ebola cases and 336 suspected cases in the DRC. There had been 88 suspected deaths in the DRC, as well as two confirmed cases and one confirmed death in Uganda from people who had traveled to the DRC. Ebola virus spreads through direct contact with the bodily fluids of an infected person and does not spread through casual contact or air. "CDC has extensive experience and expertise in responding to Ebola outbreaks," CDC acting director Dr. Jay Bhattacharya said on a call with reporters on Friday. "It is a large outbreak, and we were just informed yesterday about it." He added, "We're absolutely committed to making sure that they can get resources as they need. We have helped with other Ebola outbreaks in the past ... we have lots of hard-earned lessons. The key thing here is to know that we are absolutely involved." This is the DRC's 17th outbreak of Ebola since the disease emerged in the 1970s, according to the WHO. This strain of Ebola is caused by Bundibugyo virus, for which there are no therapeutics or vaccines, the WHO said. The WHO has declared international public health emergencies over previous Ebola outbreaks, as well as COVID-19 and mpox.
